3.2.1
) modulo gauge transformations,
\bar{S}_{H}(F)=\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)/{\sim}
and call it the
physical phase space
.

Often it is convenient to impose subsidiary conditions on field
sections, called
gauge fixing
, that restrict the choice of
representatives of gauge equivalence classes. The gauge fixing is called

full
if they only allow a unique representative from each
equivalence class, and otherwise called
partial
. The gauge
transformations that are compatible with a partial gauge fixing are
called
residual
.

Unfortunately, PDE systems with gauge invariance cannot have a
well-posed initial value problem, and hence cannot be hyperbolic. In
particular, their linearizations cannot be Green hyperbolic. However,
the addition of subsidiary conditions on field sections can make the new
PDE system equivalent to a hyperbolic one, usually with constraints. In
practice, many hyperbolic systems with constraints arise after adding
such gauge fixing conditions to a non-hyperbolic system with gauge
invariance. Interestingly, after many convenient gauge fixings, there
may remain non-trivial residual gauge freedom. For later convenience, as
we did with constraints, we restrict our attention to what we call

recognizable gauge transformations
. That is, given linearized
gauge transformations of the form
\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
and a partially gauge
fixed hyperbolic system with equation form
(\dot{f},\tilde{F}^{*})
, we
can fit them into the following commutative diagram, whose columns form
formally exact complexes:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{K}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{f}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{f}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(P^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{\prime*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{K}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{\prime*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(50)

Their adjoints fit into the adjoint diagram whose columns are also
formally exact complexes:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{K}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}^{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{f}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{G}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{f}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{s}^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{\prime*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{K}^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{\prime*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{k}^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(P^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(51)

The systems with equation forms
(\dot{k},\tilde{P}^{*})
and

(\dot{k}^{\prime*},\tilde{P}^{\prime*})
are required to be hyperbolic
and
P^{\prime}\to M
is called the
gauge invariant field bundle
, while

\dot{g}^{\prime}
is called the
operator of gauge invariant field
combinations
.

The above commutative diagrams are very similar to those used to define
parametrizable constraints in Sec.
3.2.2
. Thus, we can define
all the same cohomologies:
H^{g}_{0}(F)
,
H^{g}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
,

H^{g}_{SC}(F)
,
H^{g}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
,
H^{g}_{SC}(F,\dot{f})
defined by
diagram (
50
), and
H^{g^{*}}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
,

H^{g^{*}}_{SC}(F)
,
H^{g^{*}}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
,
H^{g^{*}}_{0}(F)
,

H^{g^{*}}_{SC}(F,\dot{f}^{*})
defined by diagram (
51
). Also,
in exactly the same way, there are bilinear pairings
\langle-,-\rangle
and
\langle-,-\rangle_{G}
defined on respective pairs of
these cohomologies. On the other hand, the following definition is not
quite analogous, reflecting how this hypothesis is used in later
sections.

Definition 3.6
.

The gauge transformations are said to be
globally recognizable
if
the natural pairing between the cohomologies
H^{g}_{SC}(F)
and

H^{g^{*}}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
, defined using the commutative
diagrams (
50
) and (
51
), is non-degenerate.

3.2.4
Formal
T
and
T^{*}
for configurations

Here we consider a section
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)\subset\mathrm{\Gamma}(F)
and examine
the formal tangent and cotangent spaces at
T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}=T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}(F)
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}=T^{*}_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}(F)
at
\phi
.

Definition 3.7
.

We define the
formal full tangent space at
\phi
as the set of
spacelike compactly supported sections and we define the
formal full
cotangent space at
\phi
as the set

T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)\quad\text{and}\quad T^{*}_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*}).

(52)

The natural pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}\times T^{*}_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}\to\mathbb{R}
is

\langle\psi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\int_{M}\psi\cdot\tilde{\alpha}^{*}.

(53)

Lemma 3.8
.

The natural pairing between
T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}
is
non-degenerate.

This is essentially the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations
and the proof is standard
[
70
, §IV.3.1]
.

Since the physical phase space will be identified with the space of
gauge orbits
\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{H}(F)
in the solution space
\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
, given a
solution section
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
, the formal tangent space

T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}=T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{H}(F)
at the corresponding
equivalence class
[\phi]\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{H}(F)
in the space of gauge orbits
consists of equivalence classes of linearized solutions up to linearized
gauge transformations. Dually, the formal cotangent space

T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{S}=T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{H}(F)
will consist of dual densities
annihilated by the adjoint of infinitesimal gauge transformation
generator.

Since gauge transformations act on field configurations and not just
solutions, it makes sense to consider all field configurations related
by gauge transformations as physically equivalent. The tangent space

T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}
will be reduced to the quotient (or physical) tangent
space
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}
and the cotangent space
T^{*}\mathrm{\Gamma}
to the
subset
T^{*}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}
of gauge invariant elements. The natural
pairing between them is shown to be non-degenerate under the condition
of global recognizability, that is, the vertical formally exact complexes in
diagrams (
50
) and (
51
) are exact. We deal with
field configurations first and delay the discussion of solutions to the
next section.

The exactness of the composition
\dot{g}^{\prime}\circ\dot{g}=0
ensures that
we can recognize pure gauge field configurations, which are of the form

\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
for some spacelike compactly supported section

\varepsilon\colon M\to P
, precisely as those spacelike compact field
sections
\psi\colon M\to F
that give vanishing gauge invariant field
combinations
\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi]=0
. On the other hand, the exactness of
the dual composition
\dot{g}^{*}\circ\dot{g}^{\prime*}=0
ensures that
we can parametrize gauge invariant, compactly supported dual densities

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\colon M\to\tilde{F}^{*}
, those satisfying

\dot{g}^{*}[\alpha]=0
, precisely as the image of the differential
operator
\dot{g}^{\prime*}
acting on compactly supported sections of

\tilde{P}^{\prime*}\to M
.

Definition 3.9
.

The
formal gauge invariant full tangent space
at
\phi
is the
set of gauge equivalence classes of
\phi
-spacelike compactly supported
sections,

\displaystyle T_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=\{[\psi]\mid\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)\},

(54)

\displaystyle[\psi]

\displaystyle\sim\psi+\dot{g}[\varepsilon],~\text{with}~\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P).

(55)

The
formal gauge invariant full cotangent space
at
\phi
is the
set of compactly supported gauge invariant dual densities,

T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}=\{\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})\mid\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0\}.

(56)

The natural pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon T_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}\times T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}\to\mathbb{R}
is

\langle[\psi],\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\int_{M}\psi\cdot\tilde{\alpha}^{*}.

(57)

Lemma 3.10
.

If the gauge transformations are globally recognizable
(Def.
3.6
), the natural pairing between the gauge invariant
spaces
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}
is non-degenerate.

Proof 3.11
.

Non-degeneracy in the second argument follows once again from the
fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations:
\langle[\psi],\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle\psi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=0

for all
\psi\in T_{\phi}\mathrm{\Gamma}
implies that
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=0
.

Non-degeneracy in the first argument is more complicated, since we can
no longer use arbitrary
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}
in the second argument. It
now requires an appeal to the global recognizability of the gauge
transformations. Suppose that
\langle[\psi],\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=0
for all
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathrm{\Gamma}}
. We need to show
that this implies
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
is pure gauge, for some
spacelike compactly supported
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
.

By definition, a gauge invariant dual density represents a cohomology
class
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]_{g^{*}}\in H^{g^{*}}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
, defined in
Sec.
3.2.3
. In fact, any such class could be represented.
Considering
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]
with arbitrary

\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{\prime*})
, we find

\langle[\psi],\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle\psi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle\psi,\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]\rangle=\langle\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi],\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\rangle.

(58)

Since
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}
could be arbitrary, the vanishing of

\langle\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi],\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\rangle
implies that

\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi]=0
. That is,
\psi
necessarily represents a
cohomology class
[\psi]_{g}\in H^{g}_{SC}(F)
, also defined in
Sec.
3.2.3
. Therefore we find that non-degeneracy in the
first argument implies that

\langle\psi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle[\psi]_{g},[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]_{g^{*}}\rangle=0,

(59)

where the last pairing is in the respective cohomologies and the class

[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]_{g^{*}}
allowed to be arbitrary. But the global
recognizability hypothesis specifies precisely that the above pairing in
cohomology is non-degenerate and implies that
[\psi]_{g}=[0]
and hence
that
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
, with
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
, is pure
gauge.

3.2.5
Formal
T
and
T^{*}
for solutions

The formal tangent space
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
will consist of linearized
solutions, that is solutions of the linearized constrained hyperbolic
system
\dot{f}[\psi]=0
and
\dot{c}[\psi]=0
. The formal cotangent
space will naturally consist of equivalence classes of dual densities up
to the images of the adjoints of
\dot{f}
and
\dot{c}
. After giving
the precise definitions below, we prove that that the natural pairing
between these formal tangent and cotangent spaces is non-degenerate.

Definition 3.12
.

We define the
formal solutions tangent space
at
\phi
as the set of
spacelike compactly supported linearized solution sections,

T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}=T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)=\{\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)\mid\dot{f}[\psi]=\dot{c}[\psi]=0\}.

(60)

We define the
formal solutions cotangent space
at
\phi
as the set
of equivalence classes of compactly supported dual densities,

\displaystyle T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}=T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)

\displaystyle=\{[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\mid\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})\},

(61)

\displaystyle[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{\alpha}^{*}+\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}],~~\text{with}~~\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F),~\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*}).

(62)

The natural pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}\times T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is

\langle\psi,[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=\int_{M}\psi\cdot\tilde{\alpha}^{*}.

(63)

As a warm-up before the main result of this section, we fist handle the
case where the constraints and gauge transformations are trivial.

Lemma 3.13
.

If the constraints
\dot{c}[\phi]=0
and the gauge transformations are
trivial, then the natural pairing between
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
is
non-degenerate.

Proof 3.14
.

Non-degeneracy in the first argument follows again from the fundamental
lemma of the calculus of variations:
\langle\psi,[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=0
for all
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
implies
that
\psi=0
.

Non-degeneracy in the second argument is more tricky, since now
\psi

can no longer be arbitrary. Suppose we have
\langle\psi,[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=0
for all spacelike compactly supported
linearized solutions
\psi\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
. From this, we need to deduce
that
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=[0]
, which means
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]
for some compactly supported
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
.

By Prp.
2.4
, we can parametrize all solutions as
\psi=\mathrm{G}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
, using unrestricted
\tilde{\beta}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
. The following simple calculation

\langle\mathrm{G}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=\langle\mathrm{G}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}],\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=-\langle\tilde{\beta}^{*},\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle

(64)

and an application of the fundamental lemma of the calculus of
variations shows that
\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
. But, once again
appealing to Prp.
2.4
, this implies that
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]
with
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
, which concludes the proof.

Remark 3.15
.

At this point, it is worth mentioning that the natural pairing between

T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
and
T_{\phi}^{*}\mathcal{S}
(again, in the absence of constraints or
gauge transformations) is essentially equivalent, via
Lem.
2.16
, to the Green pairing
(Def.
2.13
), which is also non-degenerate.

In the presence of gauge symmetries, the formal tangent space consists
of equivalence classes of linearized solutions up to gauge
transformations. On the other hand, the formal cotangent space is
restricted to equivalence represented by gauge invariant dual densities.
After giving the precise definitions below, we prove that the natural
pairing between these formal tangent and cotangent spaces is
non-degenerate, provided the constraints
\dot{c}[\phi]=0
are
globally parametrizable and the gauge transformation are globally
recognizable.

The following technical definition is motivated by following steps: we
first construct the solution space
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
and then quotient by its
purge gauge subspace.

Definition 3.16
.

We define the
formal gauge invariant solutions tangent space
at

\phi
as the set of gauge equivalence classes of
\phi
-spacelike
compactly supported linearized solution sections,

\displaystyle T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}=T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{H}(F)

\displaystyle=\{[\psi]\mid\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F),\dot{f}[\psi]=0,\dot{c}[\psi]=0\},

\displaystyle[\psi]

\displaystyle\sim\psi+\dot{g}[\varepsilon],

\displaystyle{}\quad\text{with}~\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)

\displaystyle{}\quad\text{and}~\dot{f}[\dot{g}[\varepsilon]]=0,\dot{c}[\dot{g}[\varepsilon]]=0.

(65)

The
formal gauge invariant solutions cotangent space
at
\phi
is
the set of equivalence classes of compactly supported gauge invariant
dual densities,

\displaystyle T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}=T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{H}(F)

\displaystyle=\{[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\mid\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*}),\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\dot{g}^{*}[\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}]],

\displaystyle{}\qquad\text{with}~\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F),\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*})\},

\displaystyle[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{\alpha}^{*}+\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}],

\displaystyle{}\quad\text{with}~\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F),\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*}).

(66)

The natural pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\times T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathbb{R}
is

\langle[\psi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=\int_{M}\psi\cdot\tilde{\alpha}^{*}.

(67)

We now prove the main result of this section.

Lemma 3.17
.

If the constraints
\dot{c}[\phi]=0
are globally parametrizable
(Def.
3.4
) and the gauge transformations are globally
recognizable (Def.
3.6
), then the natural pairing between

T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is non-degenerate.

Proof 3.18
.

Unfortunately, we now cannot directly rely on the fundamental lemma of the
calculus of variations to prove non-degeneracy in either argument.
Instead, we proceed roughly as in the proof of
Lem.
3.10
.

To prove non-degeneracy in the first argument, suppose we have
\langle[\psi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=0
for arbitrary

[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\in T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. It is easy to see from the
definition that we can restrict ourselves to representatives that
satisfy
\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
. Then still,

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}
may represent an arbitrary cohomology class

[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]_{g^{*}}\in H^{g^{*}}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
, defined in
Sec.
3.2.3
. We now need to show that
[\psi]=[0]
, or
equivalently that
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
for some
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
. Considering
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]
with arbitrary

\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{*})
, we have

\langle[\psi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=\langle\psi,\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]\rangle=\langle\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi],\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\rangle.

(68)

Since
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}
could be arbitrary, the vanishing of

\langle\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi],\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\rangle
implies that

\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi]=0
. That is,
\psi
necessarily represents a
cohomology class
[\psi]_{g}\in H^{g}_{SC}(F)
, also defined in
Sec.
3.2.3
. Therefore, for any
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\in T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, we find

0=\langle\psi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle[\psi]_{g},[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]_{g^{*}}\rangle,

(69)

where the last pairing is in the respective cohomologies and the class

[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]_{g^{*}}
is allowed to be arbitrary. But the global
recognizability hypothesis specifies precisely that the above pairing in
cohomology is non-degenerate and implies that
[\psi]_{g}=[0]
and hence
that
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
, with
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
, is pure
gauge.

To prove non-degeneracy in the second argument, suppose we have
\langle[\psi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=0
for arbitrary
[\psi]\in T_{\phi}\bar{S}
, which is represented by a solution of
\dot{f}[\psi]=0
,
\dot{c}[\psi]=0
. Then, from the definition, it is clear that a
solution
\psi
may also represent an arbitrary cohomology class

[\psi]_{g}\in H^{g}_{SC}(F,\dot{f})
, defined in Sec.
3.2.2
. We
now need to show that
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=[0]
, or equivalently that

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}]
for some

\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
and
\dot{\varepsilon}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*})
. We can
always choose
\psi=\dot{c}^{\prime}[\zeta^{\prime}]
with
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(E^{\prime})

such that
\dot{h^{\prime}}[\zeta^{\prime}]=0
, or equivalently
\zeta^{\prime}=\mathrm{H}^{\prime}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]
and
\psi=\dot{c}^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{H}^{\prime}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]=\mathrm{G}\circ\dot{q}^{\prime}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}]
,
with
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{\prime*})

arbitrary. We then have

\langle[\psi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=\langle\mathrm{G}\circ\dot{q}^{\prime}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}],\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=-\langle\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*},\dot{q}^{\prime*}[\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]\rangle.

(70)

Since
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*}
could be arbitrary, the vanishing of

\langle\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime*},\dot{q}^{\prime*}[\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]\rangle
implies that

\dot{q}^{\prime*}[\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]
. That is,

\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]
represents a cohomology class

[\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]_{c^{*}}\in H^{c^{*}}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*},\dot{f}^{*})
, also
defined in Sec.
3.2.2
. Therefore, for any
[\psi]\in T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, we find

0=\langle[\psi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle=\langle\psi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=-\langle\psi,\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle_{G}=-\langle[\psi]_{c},[\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]_{c^{*}}\rangle_{G},

(71)

where the last two pairings are the Green pairing
(Def.
2.13
, and Lem.
2.16
) and
its descent to the respective cohomologies. But the global
parametrizability hypothesis specifies precisely that the above pairing
is non-degenerate and implies that
[\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]_{c^{*}}=[0]
, or equivalently that there exists a
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*})
such that

\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\dot{q}^{*}\circ\mathrm{H}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}]=\mathrm{G}^{*}\circ\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}].

(72)

This, in turn, implies that
\mathrm{G}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}-\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}]]=0
. Hence, by the exact sequence of
Lem.
2.6
, we have that
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\varepsilon}^{*}]
for some
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
.

3.3
Symplectic and Poisson structure

In this section, we endow the space of solutions
\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
of a
variational PDE system with the structures of both a symplectic and a
Poisson manifold (or rather formal versions of these structures), really
turning it into the phase space of classical field theory.

In general, a variational system may have gauge symmetries. These must
be gauge fixed. The resulting system should then be put into the form of
a constrained hyperbolic system. Or, rather, what is most important for
us is that these steps can be carried out for the linearization of our
variational system (Sec.
3.3.1
). If the constraints are
(a) globally parametrizable, (b) the gauge transformations globally
recognizable and (c) the gauge fixing satisfies an extra compatibility
condition, then we can apply a generalized Forger-Romero argument
(Sec.
3.3.2
). Next, we use the covariant phase space
formalism to build the formal symplectic form
(Sec.
3.3.3
) and the Peierls formula to build the formal
Poisson bivector (Sec.
3.3.4
). Finally, we prove that
the two structures are equivalent (Sec.
3.3.5
).

For the remainder of this section, let us fix a Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}\in\mathrm{\Omega}^{n,0}(F)
. Following Sec.
3.1
, it defines a
presymplectic current density
\omega\in\mathrm{\Omega}^{n-1,2}(F)
and its
Euler-Lagrange equations define a PDE system with equation form

(\mathrm{EL},\tilde{F}^{*})
.

3.3.1
Variational systems, gauge fixing and constraints

There are many reasons why the equation form
(\mathrm{EL},\tilde{F}^{*})
of the
equations of motion of the classical field theory is not optimal for our
analysis. As we shall see later on, the Peierls formula calls for a
Green function of the linearized equations of motion. However, the
particular form of the differential operator
\mathrm{EL}
may not be one that
directly falls into one of the classes of differential operators that
are easily recognized as hyperbolic, so that its linearizations possess
Green functions. For instance, in the presence of gauge invariance, we
must first adjoin a gauge fixing condition, say
c_{g}[\phi]=0
valued
in a bundle
E_{g}\to M
. Also, in many cases, either due to the extra
gauge fixing equations or due to internal integrability conditions
(Sec.
B.1
), the equations can only be cast in
hyperbolic form with constraints. Of course, let us not forget that, a
priori, we haven’t yet restricted the choice of
\mathcal{L}
in any way that
would guarantee that its Euler-Lagrange system is not elliptic or of
some other hyperbolic type. So, we call the Euler-Lagrange system

hyperbolizable
if, after a possible gauge fixing, it can be shown
to be equivalent to a constrained hyperbolic system in a way that we
make precise below. From now on, we require that for a classical field
theory the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
is chosen such that its Euler-Lagrange
equations are hyperbolizable. We shall see later in
Sec.
4
, that many relativistic field theories of
physical interest are in fact hyperbolizable.

Consider the gauge fixed Euler-Lagrange system, whose equation form is

(\mathrm{EL}\oplus c_{g},\tilde{F}^{*}\oplus_{M}E_{g})
. It is hyperbolizable if it
equivalent (in the sense of Sec.
B.1
) to a
constrained hyperbolic system
(f\oplus c,\tilde{F}^{*}\oplus_{M}E)
.
Again, we are not going into the details of what constitutes a
non-linear constrained hyperbolic system but defer instead to
Refs.
[
75
]
and
[
60
]
. The equivalence must have the
following form:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{EL}&=R\circ(f\oplus c)\\
c_{g}&=R_{g}\circ c\\
\end{aligned}\right.\quad\iff\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} f&=\bar{R}\circ(\mathrm{EL}\oplus c_{g})\\
c&=\bar{R}_{g}\circ(\mathrm{EL}\oplus c_{g})\end{aligned}\right.,

(73)

where the
R
,
\bar{R}
,
R_{g}
and
\bar{R}_{g}
are possibly
non-linear differential operators.

As discussed earlier, in Sec.
3.2.1
, for the purposes of our
discussion, it is sufficient to pick a single dynamical linearization
point
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
and linearize the above PDE systems about it. In
particular, the linearized equations will be sufficient to define the
formal tangent and cotangent spaces
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
,
T_{\phi}^{*}\mathcal{S}
and their
gauge invariant analogs
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
,
T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. In other
words, we need to work with the linearized versions of each of the
hyperbolic system, the constraints, the Euler-Lagrange system, the gauge
fixing conditions, the gauge transformations, as well as the
hyperbolization. As before, we denote the equation form of the
linearized hyperbolic system
(\dot{f},\tilde{F}^{*})
. The linearized
constraints are presumed to be globally parametrizable and fit into the
commutative diagrams (
47
) and (
48
). The
linearized gauge transformations are presumed to be globally
recognizable and fit into the commutative diagrams (
50
)
and (
51
). The linearized
\mathrm{EL}
equations are denoted

(\mathrm{J},\tilde{F}^{*})
and are also called the
Jacobi system
,

\mathrm{J}[\psi]_{a}(x)=\mathrm{J}^{I}_{ab}\partial_{I}\phi^{b}(x)=0,

(74)

with
\mathrm{J}
the
Jacobi operator
,
[
7
]
while the
linearized gauge fixing conditions are denoted by the equation form

(\dot{c}_{g},E_{g})
. In local coordinates
(x^{i},u^{a})
on
F
, the
components of the Jacobi operator satisfy the identity

\mathrm{J}^{I}_{ab}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}u^{b}_{I}=\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}\mathrm{EL}_{a}.

(75)

The equivalence of the linearized systems takes the following form:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{J}&=r\circ\dot{f}+r_{c}\circ\dot{c}\\
\dot{c}_{g}&=r_{g}\circ\dot{c}\\
\end{aligned}\right.\quad\iff\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{f}&=\bar{r}\circ\mathrm{J}+\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}\\
\dot{c}&=\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\mathrm{J}+\bar{r}_{g}\circ\dot{c}_{g}\end{aligned}\right.

(76)

If the operator
\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}
is non-vanishing, it means that part of the
constraints consist of integrability conditions of the Jacobi system.

Note that, strictly speaking, the
r
- and
\bar{r}
- differential
operators effecting the equivalence are not inverses of each other.
Their compositions may differ from the identity by some differential
operator that factors through a differential identity, that is,

\dot{q}\circ\dot{f}-\dot{h}\circ\dot{c}=0
or
\dot{g}^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}=0
. In other words, we must have

\displaystyle r\circ\bar{r}+r_{c}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}+p_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*},

\displaystyle\bar{r}\circ r

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}+p_{f}\circ\dot{q},

(77)

\displaystyle r\circ\bar{r}_{c}+r_{c}\circ\bar{r}_{g}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bar{r}\circ r_{c}+\bar{r}_{c}\circ r_{g}

\displaystyle=-p_{f}\circ\dot{h},

(78)

\displaystyle r_{g}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}

\displaystyle=p_{g}\circ\dot{g}^{*},

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}\circ r

\displaystyle=p_{c}\circ\dot{q},

(79)

\displaystyle r_{g}\circ\bar{r}_{g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id},

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{g}\circ r_{g}+\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}\circ r_{c}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-p_{c}\circ\dot{h},

(80)

for some differential operators
p_{\mathrm{J}}
,
p_{f}
,
p_{g}
and
p_{c}
. Also,
the identity
\dot{q}\circ\dot{f}-\dot{h}\circ\dot{c}=0
, when
expressed in terms of the
\mathrm{J}
and
\dot{c}_{g}
operators, is identically
satisfied when

\displaystyle\dot{q}\circ\bar{r}-\dot{h}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}

\displaystyle=q_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*},

(81)

\displaystyle\dot{q}\circ\bar{r}_{c}-\dot{h}\circ\bar{r}_{g}

\displaystyle=0.

(82)

It is worth noting that the above relations involving the
r
- and

\bar{r}
-operators follow from the equivalence (
76
)
only when
\mathrm{J}
and
\dot{c}_{g}
satisfy no additional differential
identities. However, we will simply presume that they hold as needed
sufficient conditions for the derivation of the Peierls formulas later
in Sec.
3.3.4
.

Finally, to make sure that the condition
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0
in fact
constitutes a gauge fixing condition, we require the following
compatibility between the gauge transformation operator and the
constraints that we shall refer to as the
gauge fixing
compatibility
condition:

\dot{s}^{\prime}\circ\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}=0.

(83)

This condition connects constraints (represented by
\dot{c}_{g}
) and
gauge transformations (represented by
\dot{s}^{\prime}
). Roughly speaking,
this condition says that the part of the constraints
\dot{c}[\psi]=0

that comes from
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0
is sufficient, when adjoined to

\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0
to make the gauge fixed system hyperbolizable. This
compatibility condition becomes important later on, in
Lem.
3.26
, to show that the gauge invariant formal cotangent
space
T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
can be equivalently defined in two ways,
involving either the
\mathrm{J}
operator or the
\dot{f}
,
\dot{c}

operators. Also, it helps prove that the hyperbolic differential
operator
\dot{k}
, that acts on gauge invariant field combinations in
the presence of recognizable gauge transformations, is actually
independent of the choice of gauge fixing operator
\dot{c}_{g}
as long
as the compatibility condition is satisfied (see Cor.
3.48
).

For future reference, it is convenient to state here the following

Lemma 3.19
.

The gauge fixing compatibility condition Eq. (
83
) is equivalent
to the existence of a differential operator
\bar{r}_{s}\colon\mathrm{\Gamma}(F)\to\mathrm{\Gamma}(\tilde{P}^{*})
such that

\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}=\dot{s}\circ\bar{r}_{s}.

(84)

That is,
\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}
factors through
\dot{s}
.

Proof 3.20
.

This follows directly from the gauge compatibility
condition (
83
) and Lem.
2.10
.

We summarize the conditions listed in this section in the following

Definition 3.21
.

The Euler-Lagrange system
(\mathrm{EL},\tilde{F}^{*})
or just the Jacobi system

(\mathrm{J},\tilde{F}^{*})
is said to be
hyperbolizable
if the following
conditions are met: (a) there exists a gauge fixing and an equivalence
with a constrained hyperbolic system of the form (
73
)
or (
76
), (b) the constraints are parametrizable and the
gauge transformations are recognizable with respect to the resulting
hyperbolic subsystem, and (c) the gauge fixing compatibility
condition (
83
) is satisfied.

The consequences of hyperbolizability are explored in the following
section. We stress that these conditions are sufficient for our purposes
and can in fact be satisfied by many relativistic field theories of
physical interest, but some of the same results could also hold under
weaker conditions.

3.3.2
Causal Green functions

The goal of this section is to use the gauge fixed
equivalence (
76
) with a constrained hyperbolic system to
construct a causal Green function for the Jacobi system.

First, we show that the residual gauge transformations (those that are
still allowed by the gauge fixing condition
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0
)
essentially come from gauge parameters that satisfy the symmetric
hyperbolic equation
\dot{k}[\varepsilon]=0
. The main purpose of this lemma
is to serve as a reference argument for one of the sub-results of
Thm.
3.29
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Given a
\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
such that
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0

and
\psi\in\operatorname{im}\dot{g}
, there exists
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
such
that
\dot{k}[\varepsilon]=0
and
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
precisely when the
image of a map (to be defined in the proof)
{}^{g}\mathrm{K}\colon\ker\dot{s}\subseteq\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})\to H^{g}_{SC}(F,\dot{f})
is trivial (see
Sec.
3.2.3
).

Proof 3.23
.

Recall that the Jacobi system, due to its variational character is
easily shown to be self-adjoint:

\mathrm{J}^{*}=\mathrm{J}.

(85)

Also, gauge invariance and Noether’s second theorem imply the identities

\mathrm{J}\circ\dot{g}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{g}^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}=0.

(86)

The equivalence of the gauge fixed Jacobi system with the constrained
hyperbolic system postulated in (
76
) then gives

\dot{s}\circ\dot{k}=\dot{f}\circ\dot{g}=\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}\circ\dot{g}.

(87)

Suppose that
\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
such that
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0

and
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
. Let

\tilde{\beta}^{*}=\dot{k}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})
and note
that

\dot{s}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=\dot{s}\circ\dot{k}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]=\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}\circ\dot{g}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]=\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0.

(88)

Then also, of course,
\dot{f}[\psi]=\dot{f}\circ\dot{g}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]=\dot{s}\circ\dot{k}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]=\dot{s}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=0
. Hence,

\psi
represents a cohomology class
[\psi]_{g}\in H^{g}_{SC}(F,\dot{f})

(Sec.
3.2.3
). The conclusion of this lemma holds precisely
when this cohomology class is trivial,
[\psi]_{g}
.

Let
\{\chi_{\pm}\}
be a partition of unity adapted to a Cauchy surface
(Def.
2.5
) and recall the associated splitting map
(Lem.
2.6
)
\mathrm{K}_{\chi}\colon\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})\to\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
that inverts
\dot{k}
from the right. We can make two
observations: (a) the difference
\varepsilon^{\prime}-\mathrm{K}_{\chi}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
is in

\mathcal{S}_{SC}(P)
, and (b)
\eta_{\chi}=\dot{g}[\mathrm{K}_{\chi}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]=\mathrm{G}[\dot{s}_{\chi}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]
is in
\mathcal{S}_{SC}(F)
, where

\dot{s}_{\chi}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=\pm\dot{s}[\chi_{\pm}\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
has compact support since
\dot{s}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=0
. Hence
\eta
and
\psi
define the same cohomology class,

[\eta_{\chi}]_{g}=[\psi]_{g}
. Moreover, the choice of the adapted
partition of unity
\{\chi_{\pm}\}
doesn’t matter, since for any other
choice
\{\chi^{\prime}_{\pm}\}
the differences

(\chi^{\prime}_{\pm}-\chi_{\pm})\tilde{\beta}^{*}
have compact support, so that

[\eta_{\chi^{\prime}}]=[\eta_{\chi}]
.

Thus, the composition of maps

{}^{g}\mathrm{K}\colon\ker\dot{s}\subset\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dot{g}\circ\mathrm{K}_{\chi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{S}_{SC}(F)\to H^{g}_{SC}(F,\dot{f})

(89)

is independent of the choice of the adapted partition of unity and, as
desired, its image coincides with the image of the subset of

\mathcal{S}_{SC}(F)
consisting of elements of the form
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon^{\prime}]

with
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
. Thus, each such
\psi=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]

where
\dot{k}[\varepsilon]=0
precisely when the image of
{}^{g}\mathrm{K}
is trivial.

It was remarked in the above proof that
\mathrm{J}\circ\dot{g}=0
and

\dot{g}^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}=0
. This is actually enough information to prove
that the Jacobi operator must factor through
\dot{g}^{\prime}
on the right and
through
\dot{g}^{\prime*}
on the left.

Lemma 3.24
.

There exists a differential operator
\mathrm{J}_{g}
such that
\mathrm{J}=\mathrm{J}_{g}\circ\dot{g}^{\prime}=\dot{g}^{\prime*}\circ\mathrm{J}_{g}^{*}
.

Proof 3.25
.

This is a simple consequence of Eq. (
86
),
Lem.
2.10
, and the self-adjointness of
\mathrm{J}
.

Next, we prove a lemma that will be used to establish an alternative
characterization of the formal gauge invariant solutions cotangent space
(Def.
3.16
) in the main theorem of this section.

Lemma 3.26
.

Provided the sufficient conditions listed in Sec.
3.3.1

hold, any compactly supported dual density
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
that satisfies

\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\dot{g}^{*}[\dot{f}^{*}[\psi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\beta}]],

(90)

for some
\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
and
\tilde{\beta}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*})
, can be written as
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}[\xi]
,
for some
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
.

Proof 3.27
.

We start by defining a field section
\psi_{+}
with retarded support such
that
\dot{f}^{*}[\psi_{+}]=\dot{f}^{*}[\psi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
.
If it is simple to check that we can define
\psi_{+}=\psi+\dot{q}^{*}\circ\mathrm{H}^{*}_{+}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
. (Using advanced support would
have also been possible.) Then,

\dot{k}^{*}\circ\dot{s}^{*}[\psi_{+}]=\dot{g}^{*}\circ\dot{f}^{*}[\psi_{+}]=\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0.

(91)

Since
\dot{k}^{*}
is invertible on sections of retarded support, we must
have
\dot{s}^{*}[\psi_{+}]=0
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=\dot{f}^{*}[\psi_{+}]=\mathrm{J}^{*}\circ\bar{r}^{*}[\psi_{+}]+\dot{c}_{g}^{*}\circ\bar{r}_{c}[\psi_{+}]

(92)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{J}[\bar{r}^{*}[\psi]+\bar{r}^{*}\circ\dot{q}^{*}\circ\mathrm{H}^{*}_{+}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]+\bar{r}_{s}^{*}[\dot{s}^{*}[\psi_{+}]]

(93)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{J}[\bar{r}^{*}[\psi]+\dot{g}\circ q_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ\mathrm{H}^{*}_{+}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]+\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ(\dot{h}^{*}\circ\mathrm{H}^{*}_{+})[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]

(94)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{J}[\bar{r}^{*}[\psi]+\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]],

(95)

where we have used the equivalence (
76
), the formal
self-adjointness of
\mathrm{J}
, Lem.
3.19
, and the
identities (
81
) and
\mathrm{J}\circ\dot{g}=0
. Therefore, the
desired conclusion holds with
\xi=\bar{r}^{*}[\psi]+\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
.

Finally, we motivate the Peierls formula and then state and prove the
main theorem of this section. Equivalence with a constrained hyperbolic
system now allows us to solve the inhomogeneous problem

\mathrm{J}[\psi]=\tilde{\alpha}^{*},\quad\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0,

(96)

where the source must necessarily satisfy the gauge invariance condition

\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
. The equivalent inhomogeneous problem in
symmetric hyperbolic form is

\dot{f}[\psi]=\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}],\quad\dot{c}[\psi]=\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}].

(97)

Recall from Lem.
2.8
that this system is solvable iff
the sources satisfy the consistency identity:

\dot{q}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]-\dot{h}[\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]=p_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0,

(98)

which is obviously satisfied, after using identity (
81
), for any gauge
invariant source. The retarded and advanced solutions to this
inhomogeneous problem are then
\psi_{\pm}=\mathrm{G}_{\pm}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]
. This means that

\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\dot{c}[\mathrm{G}_{\pm}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]
and, in particular,

\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0
. Motivated by this formula, we introduce the
following retarded, advanced and causal Green functions for the gauge
fixed Jacobi system.

Definition 3.28
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{\pm}=\mathrm{G}_{\pm}\circ\bar{r}
. The
Peierls formula
is

\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{E}_{+}-\mathrm{E}_{-}=\mathrm{G}\circ\bar{r}.

(99)

We also call
\mathrm{E}
the
Peierls
or
Jacobi causal Green
function
.

One can immediately check that
\psi=\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]
satisfies
both
\dot{f}[\psi]=0
and
\dot{c}[\psi]=0
, whenever

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}
is a gauge invariant dual density. By the
equivalence (
76
), the same solution also satisfies

\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0
and
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0
.

Theorem 3.29
.

Provided the gauge fixed Jacobi system
\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0
,
\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0
is hyperbolizable (Def.
3.21
), the Jacobi causal Green
function
\mathrm{E}
defined in Eq. (
99
) fits into the following
commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{J}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{J}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{g}^{*}}
\textstyle{0,}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})}

(100)

which becomes a complex (successive arrows compose to
0
) after taking
the vertical cohomologies. That complex is exact at

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
and
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
, while at
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
and

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
its cohomologies coincide respectively with

H^{g}_{0}(F)
and
H^{g^{*}}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
.

Moreover, with reference to Def.
3.16
, we have the
isomorphisms
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\cong\ker\mathrm{J}/\operatorname{im}\dot{g}
, at

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
, and
T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\cong\ker\dot{g}^{*}/\operatorname{im}\mathrm{J}
, at

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
.

Finally, a Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset M
and a partition of unity

\{\chi_{\pm}\}
adapted to it define the following splittings at

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
and
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
:

\ker\dot{g}^{*}\cong\operatorname{im}\mathrm{J}\oplus T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{coker}\dot{g}\cong T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\operatorname{im}\dot{g}^{\prime*},

(101)

where (cf. Def.
2.5
, Lem.
2.6
, and
Def.
3.12
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\chi}\colon

\displaystyle T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}\to\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]

\displaystyle=\pm\mathrm{J}[\chi_{\pm}\psi],

(102)

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\chi}\colon

\displaystyle\operatorname{im}\dot{g}^{\prime*}\to\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F),

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\chi}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{*}\circ\mathrm{G}^{*}_{\chi}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}].

(103)

The conclusion of the theorem is rather dense with information, so its
proof is somewhat lengthy. However it simply consists of checking the
properties of the horizontal sequence in the above diagram at each of
its objects.

Proof 3.30
.

The fact that successive maps compose to zero, after taking the vertical
cohomologies, is established in items (2) and (3) below, which also
prove exactness of the resulting complex at
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
and

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
. On the other hand, cohomologies at
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
and

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
are computed in items (1) and (4).

The isomorphism
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\cong\ker\mathrm{J}/\operatorname{im}\dot{g}
is established
as follows: on the one hand, it is obvious that
\dot{f}[\psi]=0
,

\dot{c}[\psi]=0
implies
\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0
; on the other hand, item (3)
shows that both
\dot{f}\circ\mathrm{E}=0
and
\dot{c}\circ\mathrm{E}=0
, while

\operatorname{im}\mathrm{E}=\ker\mathrm{J}\pmod{\operatorname{im}\dot{g}}
. The isomorphism

T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\cong\ker\dot{g}^{*}/\operatorname{im}\mathrm{J}
is established as
follows: on the one hand, by the equivalence (
76
), it is
obvious that
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}+\mathrm{J}[\xi]
, with

\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
and
\xi
arbitrary, represents a
unique element of
T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{S}
; on the other hand, if

\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\dot{g}^{*}[\dot{f}^{*}[\psi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]
, then
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}-\dot{f}^{*}[\psi]-\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]
in

T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and Lem.
3.26
shows that any representative
of
[0]\in T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
represents only
[0]\in\ker\dot{g}^{*}/\operatorname{im}\mathrm{J}
.

Finally, the splittings (
101
), with the corresponding
splitting map identities, are established in items (3) and (4).

Note that below we make liberal use of various maps defined using the
adapted partition of unity
\{\chi_{\pm}\}
introduced in the hypothesis
of the theorem (cf. Lem.
2.6
).

1.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
, then
\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0
is equivalent to

\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi]=0
.

If
\psi\in\ker\dot{g}^{\prime}
, then by Lem.
3.24
(
\mathrm{J}=\mathrm{J}_{g}\circ\dot{g}^{\prime}
) we certainly have
\psi\in\ker\mathrm{J}
. On the other
hand, if
\psi\in\ker\mathrm{J}
, then

\dot{k}^{\prime}[\dot{g}^{\prime}[\psi]]=\dot{s}^{\prime}[\dot{f}[\psi]]=\dot{s}^{\prime}[\bar{r}\circ\mathrm{J}[\psi]+\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]]=\dot{s}^{\prime}\circ\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}[\psi]=0,

(104)

where the last equality holds due to the gauge fixing compatibility
condition (
83
) and we have also used the
equivalence (
76
). But, since
\dot{k}^{\prime}
is injective
on
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(P^{\prime})
, this can only be if
\psi\in\ker\dot{g}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, after taking vertical cohomologies, the cohomology
of (
100
) at
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
is isomorphic to

H^{g}_{0}(F)
.

2.

At
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
, we have
\mathrm{E}\circ\mathrm{J}=0\pmod{\operatorname{im}\dot{g}}
. Any
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})

such that
\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
and
\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
, with
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
, can be written as

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}[\xi]
, with
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
.

For the first part, recall the equivalent form (
84
) of
the gauge fixing compatibility condition. Direct calculation, with

\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
, then gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{J}[\xi]]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{G}[\bar{r}\circ\mathrm{J}[\xi]]

(105)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{G}[\dot{f}[\xi]-\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g}[\xi]]

(106)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{G}[\dot{s}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\xi]]

(107)

\displaystyle=\dot{g}[\mathrm{K}[\bar{r}_{s}[\xi]]].

(108)

For the second part, let
\psi=\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}]=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
and
\tilde{\beta}^{*}=\dot{k}[\varepsilon]\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{*})
. Note that
\dot{f}[\psi]=\dot{f}\circ\mathrm{G}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]=0
and also
\dot{s}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=\dot{s}\circ\dot{k}[\varepsilon]=\dot{f}\circ\dot{g}[\varepsilon]=0
. Using
the same logic and notation as in the proof of
Lem.
3.22
, we can write
\psi=\mathrm{G}[\dot{s}_{\chi}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]+\dot{s}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]]=\mathrm{G}\circ\dot{s}[\chi_{+}\tilde{\beta}^{*}+\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]
, for some

\tilde{\gamma}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{P}^{*})
. Note that the argument of

\mathrm{G}
has compact support. Recalling the definition
\psi=\mathrm{G}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}]]
and the fact that
\ker\mathrm{G}=\operatorname{im}\dot{f}
,
there must exist a
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
such that

\dot{f}[\xi]=\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]-\dot{s}[\chi_{+}\tilde{\beta}^{*}+\tilde{\gamma}^{*}].

(109)

By uniqueness of solutions with retarded support, we must have
\xi=\mathrm{G}_{+}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]-\dot{s}[\chi_{+}\tilde{\beta}^{*}+\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]]=\mathrm{G}_{+}[\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]-\dot{g}\circ\mathrm{K}_{+}[\chi_{+}\tilde{\beta}^{*}+\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]
(we could
have also used
\mathrm{G}_{-}
with the substitution
\chi_{+}\to-\chi_{-}
).
Then, direct calculation shows

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}[\xi]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{J}[\mathrm{G}_{+}\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]-\dot{g}\circ\mathrm{K}_{+}[\chi_{+}\tilde{\beta}^{*}+\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]]

(110)

\displaystyle=r\circ(\dot{f}\circ\mathrm{G}_{+})\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+r_{c}\circ(\dot{c}\circ\mathrm{G}_{+})\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(111)

\displaystyle=(r\circ\bar{r})[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+r_{c}\circ\mathrm{H}_{+}\circ(\dot{q}\circ\bar{r})[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(112)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}+p_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*}-r_{c}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}})[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+r_{c}\circ\mathrm{H}_{+}\circ(\dot{h}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}+q_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*})[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(113)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}^{*}-r_{c}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+r_{c}\circ(\mathrm{H}_{+}\circ\dot{h})\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(114)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}^{*},

(115)

where we have used identities
\mathrm{J}\circ\dot{g}=0
, (
77
)
(
81
) and the commutative diagram (
47
).

3.

At
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
, we have
\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}=0
when restricted to

\ker\dot{g}^{*}
. Any
\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
such that
\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0

can be written as
\psi=\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
, with

\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
.

For the first part, with
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})

and
\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
, direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\dot{f}\circ\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle=(\dot{f}\circ\mathrm{G})\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0,

(116)

\displaystyle\dot{c}\circ\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle=(\dot{c}\circ\mathrm{G})\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\mathrm{H}\circ(\dot{q}\circ\bar{r})[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(117)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{H}\circ\dot{h})\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+\mathrm{H}\circ q_{\mathrm{J}}[\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]]=0,

(118)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle=(r\circ(\dot{f}\circ\mathrm{E})+r_{c}\circ(\dot{c}\circ\mathrm{E}))[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}],

(119)

where we have used the identity (
81
) and exactness of
the sequence in Prp.
2.4
.

For the second part, let
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]=\pm\mathrm{J}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]
, so that
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{F}^{*})
. We claim that
\psi^{\prime}=\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

differs from
\psi
only by a pure gauge term
\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
, with

\varepsilon\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
. We examine closely the following expression,
which appears in the definition of
\psi^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\bar{r}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]

\displaystyle=\pm[(\bar{r}\circ r)\circ\dot{f}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]+(\bar{r}\circ r_{c})\circ\dot{c}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]]

(120)

\displaystyle=\pm[(\mathrm{id}+p_{f}\circ\dot{q})\circ\dot{f}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]-(\bar{r}_{c}\circ r_{g}+p_{f}\circ\dot{h})\circ\dot{c}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]]

(121)

\displaystyle=\pm[\dot{f}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]-(\bar{r}_{c}\circ\dot{c}_{g})[\chi_{\pm}\psi]+p_{f}\circ(\dot{q}\circ\dot{f}-\dot{h}\circ\dot{c})[\chi_{\pm}\psi]]

(122)

\displaystyle=\pm(\dot{f}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]-\dot{s}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]),

(123)

where we have used the identities (
77
)
and (
78
). Note that, while the above expression has
compact support, the two individual terms do not. It is important to
be able to decompose this expression in two different ways: into terms
having retarded support (
+
) or advanced support (
-
). Direct
calculation then shows

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{G}[\bar{r}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]]

(124)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{G}_{+}[\dot{f}[\chi_{+}\psi]-\dot{s}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{+}\psi]]-\mathrm{G}_{-}[-\dot{f}[\chi_{-}\psi]+\dot{s}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{-}\psi]]

(125)

\displaystyle=(\chi_{+}+\chi_{-})\psi-(\mathrm{G}_{+}\circ\dot{s})\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{+}\psi]-(\mathrm{G}_{-}\circ\dot{s})\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{-}\psi]

(126)

\displaystyle=\psi-\dot{g}[\mathrm{K}_{+}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{+}\psi]+\mathrm{K}_{-}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{-}\psi]].

(127)

Therefore, the desired conclusion holds,
\psi^{\prime}=\mathrm{E}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
, with
\varepsilon=\mathrm{K}_{+}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{+}\psi]+\mathrm{K}_{-}\circ\bar{r}_{s}[\chi_{-}\psi]\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P)
.

4.

If
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
, then

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}[\psi]
, for some
\psi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
, is
equivalent to
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
,
for some
\tilde{\beta}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{P}^{\prime*})
.

If
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}[\psi]
, then by Lem.
3.24
(
\mathrm{J}=\dot{g}^{\prime*}\circ\mathrm{J}_{g}^{*}
) we certainly have
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\in\operatorname{im}\dot{g}^{\prime*}
. On the other hand, if
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]
, let
\xi=\mathrm{K}^{\prime*}_{\chi}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(P^{\prime})
. Direct
calculation then shows

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}[\bar{r}^{*}\circ\dot{s}^{\prime*}[\xi]]

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{J}^{*}\circ\bar{r}^{*})\circ\dot{s}^{\prime*}[\xi]

(128)

\displaystyle=\dot{f}^{*}\circ\dot{s}^{\prime*}[\xi]-(\dot{c}_{g}^{*}\circ\bar{r}_{c}^{*}\circ\dot{s}^{\prime*})[\xi]

(129)

\displaystyle=\dot{g}^{\prime*}\circ(\dot{k}^{\prime*}\circ\mathrm{K}^{\prime*}_{\chi})[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]

(130)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}^{*},

(131)

where we have used the formal adjoint versions of
Eqs. (
76
) and the gauge fixing compatibility
condition (
83
). Therefore, the desired conclusion holds, with

\psi=\bar{r}^{*}\circ\dot{s}^{\prime*}[\xi]\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(F)
.
Simplifying the last expression, we get
\psi=\bar{r}^{*}\circ(\dot{s}^{\prime*}\circ\mathrm{K}^{\prime*}_{\chi})[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]=\bar{r}^{*}\circ\mathrm{G}^{*}_{\chi}[\dot{g}^{\prime*}[\tilde{\beta}^{*}]]=\bar{r}^{*}\circ\mathrm{G}^{*}_{\chi}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]
and hence

\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\chi}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\mathrm{J}\circ\bar{r}^{*}\circ\mathrm{G}^{*}_{\chi}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=\tilde{\alpha}^{*}.

(132)

Therefore, we have established that, after taking vertical
cohomologies, the cohomology of (
100
) at

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
is isomorphic to

H^{g^{*}}_{SC}(\tilde{F}^{*})
.

Remark 3.31
.

The hypotheses of Thm.
3.29
make use of the notion of
hyperbolizability (Def.
3.21
), which in turn requires the
corresponding constraints to be parametrizable (Sec.
3.2.2
)
and the gauge transformations to be recognizable (Sec.
3.2.3
).
But only the local versions of these were used. That is,
Thm.
3.29
holds even if
global
parametrizability
and recognizability fail. The global conditions instead will appear in
the representation (
161
) of the formal presymplectic form
with respect to the natural pairing between the formal tangent and
cotangent spaces.

As mentioned before, it is easy to see from its variational nature that
the Jacobi operator is self-adjoint
\mathrm{J}^{*}=\mathrm{J}
. If it were directly
invertible, the Green functions
\mathrm{E}_{\pm}
would satisfy the same
relation with their adjoints as shown in Sec. (
2.5
),
making the causal Green function anti-self-adjoint,
(\mathrm{E})^{*}=-\mathrm{E}
.
However, due to gauge invariance the relation of the gauge fixed Green
functions to their adjoints is more complicated.

Lemma 3.32
.

When restricted to act on gauge invariant dual densities, the causal
Green function of the gauge fixed Jacobi system is anti-self-adjoint up
to gauge:

(\mathrm{E})^{*}=-\mathrm{E}\pmod{\operatorname{im}\dot{g}}.

(133)

Proof 3.33
.

First, note that from identities (
76
)
and (
77
) we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}(r\circ\dot{f}+r_{c}\circ\dot{c})\circ\mathrm{G}_{\pm}\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(134)

\displaystyle=r\circ\bar{r}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]+r_{c}\circ\bar{r}_{\mathrm{J}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]

(135)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}+p_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*})[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}].

(136)

It then follows that

\displaystyle(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}\circ(\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm})=(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}+(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*},

(137)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=((\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}^{*})\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm}=\mathrm{E}_{\pm}+\dot{g}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm},

(138)

\displaystyle\text{and hence}\quad\mathrm{E}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}+(\mathrm{E}_{\mp})^{*}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}\circ\dot{g}^{*}-\dot{g}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ\mathrm{E}_{\pm}.

(139)

Given that
\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{E}_{+}-\mathrm{E}_{-}
, we then have

\mathrm{E}=-(\mathrm{E})^{*}-\dot{g}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ\mathrm{E}-(\mathrm{E})^{*}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ\dot{g}^{*},

(140)

which gives the desired conclusion.

We conclude this section by drawing attention to the fact that the kind
of gauge fixing that features in a hyperbolization, as discussed in
Sec.
3.3.1
is a special kind of partial gauge fixing. We
refer to it as
purely hyperbolic
. Any further gauge fixing
conditions are then called
residual
. We leave the consideration
of residual gauge fixing to future work. A principal difficulty in
dealing with residual gauge fixing conditions is that the resulting
constraints are no longer parametrizable (such as operators that are
elliptic on a family of spatial slices). Thus, the kernel of the gauge
fixing conditions may contain very few, if any solutions with spacelike
compact support, which would be difficult to fit into the current formal
framework for tangent and cotangent spaces to the space of solutions.

3.3.3
Formal symplectic structure

Below, we construct a formal symplectic form
\bar{\Omega}
using the
covariant phase space formalism. That is, we will integrate the
presymplectic current density
\omega
, derived in
Sec.
3.1
, over a Cauchy surface. Any Cauchy surface would
do, giving the same result. The resulting form can in general be
degenerate, though, and only becomes symplectic once projected to the
gauge invariant formal tangent space
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

Definition 3.34
.

Consider a variational system (Sec.
3.1
) with presymplectic form
\omega\in\mathrm{\Omega}^{n-1,2}(F)
(
A
). Suppose that it is hyperbolizable
(Sec.
3.3.1
) and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
is a background
solution with good causal behavior (Sec.
3.2.1
), so that the
linearized equations of motion endow
M
with a globally hyperbolic
causal structure (
C
). Then, given a Cauchy surface

\Sigma\subset M
, we define the formal
presymplectic

2
-form

\Omega
on the formal tangent space
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
by the formula

\Omega(\psi,\xi)=\int_{\Sigma}\omega[\psi,\xi]=\int_{\Sigma}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}[\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\psi}}\omega].

(141)

Recall that for any section
\psi,\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}(F)
we can define the
prolonged evolutionary vector fields
\hat{\psi},\hat{\xi}
on
J^{\infty}F)
(
A
), which can be then be contracted with
\omega\in\mathrm{\Omega}^{*}(J^{\infty}F)
.

Ideally, we would now show that
\Omega
defines a smooth, closed
differential form on the possibly infinite dimensional space of
solutions
\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
. However, we would then need to make explicit use of
the infinite dimensional differential structure on
\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
and

T\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
, which we have consistently avoided doing in this review,
preferring a formal approach, with minimal analytical details. So
instead, we will settle for showing that it is
formally smooth

and
closed
. These names are simply place holders for the
identities demonstrated in the proof of the following

Lemma 3.35
.

Under the hypotheses of Def.
3.34
, the definition of

\Omega
is independent of the choice of Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset M
.
Moreover,
\Omega
is formally closed.

Proof 3.36
.

First, we note that if
\chi,\xi\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
then the integral defining

\Omega
is necessarily finite, since the integrand
\omega[\chi,\xi]=(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}[\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}\omega]
has spacelike
compact support as both
\chi
and
\xi
do. Independence of the choice
of
\Sigma
follows if we can show that
\omega[\chi,\xi]
is de Rham
closed on
M
. This follows directly from the horizontal, on-shell
closedness of
\omega
in the variational bicomplex
(Lem.
3.1
):

\mathrm{d}[(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}\omega]=(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{h}}[\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}\omega]=(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}[\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{h}}\omega]=0.

(142)

For a fixed background solution
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
, the formal
2
-form

\Omega(\chi,\xi)
is defined as a Cauchy surface integral of a
bidifferential operator
\omega[\chi,\xi]
, which is defined by a form

\omega\in\Omega^{*}(J^{\infty}F)
. Hence we are happy to declare
\Omega
to
be
formally smooth
in its dependence on
\phi
, as long as

\omega
itself is smooth, which it is by construction. Also, in this
simple case, we are justified in declaring the formal de Rham
differential
\delta
on
\mathcal{S}_{H}(F)
to act on
\Omega
as the vertical
differential
\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}
under the integral sign. Therefore, in this context,
it is straight forward to check that
\Omega
is
formally closed

since
\omega
is on-shell, vertically closed (Lem.
3.1
):

\displaystyle(\delta\Omega)(\chi,\xi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}[\iota_{\hat{\psi}}\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}\omega]=\int_{\Sigma}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}[\iota_{\hat{\psi}}\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}\omega]

(143)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}[\iota_{\hat{\psi}}\iota_{\hat{\xi}}\iota_{\hat{\chi}}(\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}\omega)]=0.

(144)

This concludes the proof.

Though this was not attempted in Refs.
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
,
their rigorous setting for infinite dimensional geometry can be used to
remove the formal character of the above lemma. Also, it is quite clear
from the proof that the integration surface
\Sigma
in the definition
of
\Omega
need not actually be a Cauchy surface. It need only be in
the same homology
2
2

2

The appropriate homology theory here should correspond to a
variant of locally finite Borel-Moore homology, where one considers
only chains whose intersection with every spacelike compact set
is compact. This variant does not appear to have gotten any attention
in the literature and thus deserves further study.
class as a Cauchy surface. In particular, it is enough that
\Sigma

coincides with some Cauchy surface outside a compact set.

A bilinear form defines a linear map from a vector space to its
algebraic dual. A similar statement holds for a continuous bilinear form
and the topological dual space. However, our formal cotangent spaces

T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are neither the algebraic nor the
topological duals of the formal tangent spaces
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
and

T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. Thus we have to check this property for
\Omega
by
hand. This will be accomplished using one of the splitting maps for the
Jacobi system from Thm.
3.29
, which is analogous
Lem.
2.6
for hyperbolic systems. The argument in the
proof was inspired by Sec. 3.3 of Ref.
[
10
]
and Lem. 3.2.1 of
Ref.
[
64
]
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Provided the constraints are globally parametrizable
(Sec.
3.2.2
) and the gauge transformations are globally
recognizable (Sec.
3.2.3
), the presymplectic form
\Omega

defines the following map from the formal tangent space to the formal
cotangent space:

\displaystyle\Omega\colon

\displaystyle T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}\to T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}

(145)

\displaystyle\psi\mapsto[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}],

(146)

\displaystyle\text{with}~~\tilde{\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]=\pm\mathrm{J}[\chi_{\pm}\psi],

(147)

where
\mathrm{J}\colon\mathrm{\Gamma}(F)\to\mathrm{\Gamma}(\tilde{F}^{*})
the Jacobi differential
operator and
\{\chi_{\pm}\}
is a partition of unity adapted to a Cauchy
surface
\Sigma
.

Proof 3.38
.

Using the adapted partition of unity, we can write any spacelike
compactly supported solution
\psi
of
\dot{f}[\psi]=0
as
\psi=\psi_{+}+\psi_{-}
, with
\psi_{\pm}=\chi_{\pm}\psi
now being of retarded
and advanced supports. If
\psi
also satisfies the constraints

\dot{c}[f]=0
, then by Thm.
3.29
it also satisfies the
Jacobi equation
\mathrm{J}[\psi]=0
. Hence
\mathrm{J}[\psi_{+}+\psi_{-}]=0
or

\mathrm{J}[\psi_{+}]=-\mathrm{J}[\psi_{-}]=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]
. Note that the support of

\mathrm{J}[\psi_{\pm}]
is compact, since
\psi_{\pm}
satisfy the Jacobi equation
away from the intersection
S^{+}\cap S^{-}\cap\operatorname{supp}\psi
, which is by
hypothesis compact.

Next, we want to find a compactly supported dual density

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}
that satisfies
\Omega(\xi,\psi)=\langle\xi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle
for any
\xi\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
, which in
particular satisfies
\mathrm{J}[\xi]=0
. Recall that an adapted partition of
unity also depends on two additional Cauchy surfaces
\Sigma^{\pm}\subset I^{\pm}(\Sigma)
and the supports of the partition are contained in

\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\pm}\subseteq S^{\pm}=I^{\pm}(\Sigma^{\mp})
. The following direct
calculation helps us identify
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}
.

\displaystyle\Omega(\xi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\omega(\xi,\psi)[\phi]=\sum_{\pm}\int_{\Sigma}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\omega(\xi,\psi_{\pm})

(148)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\int_{\Sigma^{\mp}}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\omega(\xi,\psi_{\pm})+\sum_{\pm}\pm\int_{S^{\pm}\cap I^{\mp}(\Sigma)}\mathrm{d}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\omega(\xi,\psi_{\pm})

(149)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\pm\int_{I^{\mp}(\Sigma)}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}(\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{h}}\omega)(\xi,\psi_{\pm})

(150)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\pm\int_{I^{\mp}(\Sigma)}(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}(-\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}\mathrm{EL}_{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{v}}u^{a})(\xi,\psi_{\pm})

(151)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\mp\int_{I^{\mp}(\Sigma)}[(\mathrm{J}^{I}_{ab}\partial_{I}\xi^{b})\psi_{\pm}^{a}-(\mathrm{J}^{I}_{ab}\partial_{I}\psi_{\pm}^{b})\xi^{a}]

(152)

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{-}(\Sigma)}\xi\cdot\mathrm{J}[\psi_{+}]-\int_{I^{+}(\Sigma)}\xi\cdot\mathrm{J}[\psi_{-}]

(153)

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{-}(\Sigma)}\xi\cdot\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]-\int_{I^{+}(\Sigma)}\xi\cdot(-\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi])

(154)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\xi\cdot\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]

(155)

Note that after the integration by parts, the boundary integrals over

\Sigma^{\pm}
were dropped since they did not intersect the support of
their integrands. Then, since
\operatorname{supp}\psi_{\pm}\subseteq S^{\pm}
, the
integration over
S^{\pm}\cap I^{\mp}(\Sigma)
was extended to all of

I^{\mp}(\Sigma)
. Finally, the term
\psi_{\pm}\cdot\mathrm{J}[\xi]
was dropped
since
\xi
is a linearized solution.

To complete the proof, we use the non-degeneracy of the natural pairing
between
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
(Lem.
3.17
,
which we can invoke because of the global parametrizability and
recognizability hypotheses) to define the operator
\Omega
by the
formula

\langle\xi,\Omega\psi\rangle=\Omega(\xi,\psi)=\langle\xi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle\xi,[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle,

(156)

so that
\Omega\psi=[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\in T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
, with

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]
.

Corollary 3.39
.

Provided the constraints are globally parametrizable
(Sec.
3.2.2
) and the gauge transformations are globally
recognizable (Sec.
3.2.3
), the
2
-form
\Omega
on
T_{\phi}\mathcal{S}

projects to a
2
-form
\bar{\Omega}
on
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and hence defines
a map

\displaystyle\bar{\Omega}\colon

\displaystyle T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\to T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}

(157)

\displaystyle[\psi]\mapsto[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}],

(158)

\displaystyle\text{with}~~\tilde{\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi].

(159)

Moreover, this map is independent of the choice of adapted partition of
unity
\{\chi_{\pm}\}
.

Proof 3.40
.

Notice that in the presence of gauge symmetries (residual gauge freedom
is present after a purely hyperbolic gauge fixing) the form
\Omega
is
degenerate, since every pure gauge solution lies in its kernel:

\Omega(\dot{g}[\varepsilon],\psi)=\langle\dot{g}[\varepsilon],\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]\rangle=\pm\langle\varepsilon,\dot{g}^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}[\chi_{\pm}\psi]\rangle=0

(160)

for any
\psi
, since Noether’s second theorem implies
[
3
]
that

\dot{g}^{*}\circ\mathrm{J}=0
. So, the first part is established.

For the second part, recall that we are not interested in the dual
density
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]
specifically, which
explicitly depends on the adapted partition of unity, but rather the
equivalence class
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\in T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}
, which is defined
modulo
\operatorname{im}\dot{f}^{*}
and
\operatorname{im}\dot{c}^{*}
. Equivalently, following a
conclusion of Thm.
3.29
, since we actually want

[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\in T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\cong T^{*}_{\phi}\mathcal{S}/\operatorname{im}\dot{g}
, it is enough to consider
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}
modulo
\operatorname{im}\mathrm{J}
.
Consider another adapted partition of unity
\{\chi^{\prime}_{\pm}\}
. Because
each partition of unity provides a splitting map
(Thm.
3.29
), if we consider equivalence classes of
solutions modulo
\operatorname{im}\dot{g}
, we have
[\psi]=[\mathrm{E}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]]=[\mathrm{E}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\chi^{\prime}}[\psi]]
. Then,

[\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{J}_{\chi^{\prime}}[\psi]-\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]]]=[\psi]-[\psi]=[0]
. So, by
exactness of the sequence in Thm.
3.29
,
\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]

and
\mathrm{J}_{\chi^{\prime}}[\psi]
must differ by an element of
\operatorname{im}\mathrm{J}
; in other
words, they represent the same equivalence class in
T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

Finally, the projected map
\bar{\Omega}\colon T_{\phi}\bar{S}\to T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{S}
is defined by the formula

\langle[\xi],\bar{\Omega}[\psi]\rangle=\langle\xi,\tilde{\alpha}^{*}\rangle=\langle[\xi],[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\rangle,

(161)

with
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}=\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\psi]
, which is sufficient because the
natural pairing between
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{S}
is
non-degenerate (Lem.
3.17
, again which we can
invoke by the global parametrizability and recognizability hypotheses).

So, formally, the quotient projection to the physical phase space
effects a presymplectic reduction
(\mathcal{S}_{H}(F),\Omega)\to(\bar{S}_{H}(F),\bar{\Omega})
. We shall see later on that
\bar{\Omega}
is
non-degenerate and hence symplectic.

Remark 3.41
.

Note that the relation between
\bar{\Omega}
as a bilinear form on the
formal tangent space
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and the linear map
\mathrm{J}_{\chi}\colon T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\to T_{\phi}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
relies on the non-degeneracy of the
natural pairing between the formal tangent and cotangent spaces. This
non-degeneracy, as proven in Sec.
3.2.5
, relies on the
cohomological conditions that we call
global parametrizability

and
global recognizability
(Secs.
3.2.2

and
3.2.3
). It is clear that, if these conditions fail and
hence the natural pairing is degenerate, the form

\bar{\Omega}(\psi,\xi)=\langle\psi,\mathrm{J}_{\chi}[\xi]
may be degenerate,
even if the operator
\mathrm{J}_{\chi}
is not. This is bound to happen, because,
as one of the conclusions of Thm.
3.29
,
\mathrm{J}_{\chi}
is
invertible under the weaker hypotheses of
local parametrizability

and
local recognizability
. Such a degeneracy has already been
noted, for instance, in Refs.
[
34
]
and
[
76
]
.

3.3.4
Formal Poisson bivector, Peierls formula

Below, we construct a formal
Poisson bivector

\Pi
, using the
Peierls formula

\Pi=\mathrm{E},

(162)

where
\mathrm{E}
is again the causal Green function of the Jacobi operator

\mathrm{J}
as defined in Sec.
3.3.1
. To show that
\Pi
is
indeed a Poisson bivector, it suffices to show that (a) it is an
antisymmetric bilinear form on the formal cotangent space, (b) it
defines a map from the formal cotangent space to the formal tangent
space and (c) it is a two-sided inverse of
\bar{\Omega}
defined in
Cor.
3.39
. We actually postpone part (c) to
Sec.
3.3
. The fact that
\Pi
defines a Poisson bracket,
with its Leibniz and Jacobi identities, then formally follows from
standard arguments.

Lemma 3.42
.

The Peierls formula specifies a map from the formal cotangent space to
the formal tangent space:

\displaystyle\Pi\colon

\displaystyle T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}\to T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}

(163)

\displaystyle[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\mapsto[\psi],\quad\text{with}\quad\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0

(164)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\psi={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}].

(165)

Proof 3.43
.

The challenge is to show that
\Pi
maps equivalence classes to
equivalence classes (Def.
3.16
). That is, that any representative

\tilde{\alpha}^{*}+\dot{f}^{*}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]
of an
equivalence class
[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]\in T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, with

\dot{g}^{*}[\tilde{\alpha}^{*}]=0
, gets mapped to the same equivalence
class in
T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. By linearity, it suffices to check that
[0]\in T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is mapped to
[0]\in T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. Recall that any
solution representing
[0]\in T_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is pure gauge

\dot{g}[\varepsilon]
. Note that the equivalence (
76
) of the

(\dot{f}\oplus\dot{c},\tilde{F}^{*}\oplus E)
and
(\mathrm{J}\oplus\dot{c}_{g},\tilde{F}^{*}\oplus E_{g})
equation forms, together with the
self-adjointness of the Jacobi operator
\mathrm{J}^{*}=\mathrm{J}
, allows us to
rewrite any representative of
[0]\in T^{*}_{\phi}\bar{\mathcal{S}}
as
\mathrm{J}[\xi]+\dot{c}_{g}^{*}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]
, for some
\xi\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(F)
and

\tilde{\gamma}^{*}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}_{0}(\tilde{E}^{*}_{g})
. This representative will
also satisfy the identity

\dot{g}^{*}\circ\dot{c}^{*}_{g}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]=\dot{g}^{*}[\mathrm{J}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}_{g}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]]=0.

(166)

Direct calculation then shows that

\displaystyle\Pi[\mathrm{J}[\xi]+\dot{c}^{*}_{g}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}\circ\mathrm{J}[\xi]+\mathrm{E}\circ\dot{c}^{*}_{g}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]

(167)

\displaystyle=\dot{g}[\varepsilon]-(\dot{c}_{g}\circ\mathrm{E})^{*}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]-\dot{g}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}^{*}\circ\mathrm{E}\circ\dot{c}^{*}_{g}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]

(168)

\displaystyle\qquad{}-(\mathrm{E})^{*}\circ p_{\mathrm{J}}[\dot{g}^{*}\circ\dot{c}^{*}_{g}[\tilde{\gamma}^{*}]]
